[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. Like, not just remembering it, not like when a song gets stuck in your head—actually hearing him, clear as anything, like he’s right behind me on the trail or in my painting room.\n\nAnd I know—part of me knows he’s dead. I do know that. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like a memory. It feels completely real. I’ll turn around expecting him to be there, and then… well, he isn’t. It’s been scaring me, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It sort of started after Garrett died, and since then everything’s been… unraveling, I guess. Time’s been weird, honestly—like I keep thinking it was last week and then realizing no, it’s been weeks. But yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: What is Garrett's voice saying when you hear it?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s his voice. Garrett’s voice. Clear, like he’s right there behind me—sometimes on the trail, sometimes when I’m in the painting room.\n\nI don’t know if I can even always tell you the exact words, because the part that hits me is how real it sounds. Like not a thought, not me remembering him. I’ll turn around expecting him to be standing there, and then there’s nothing. And I know he’s dead, I do know that, but in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you see or feel anything unusual when you hear Garrett's voice, or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really… see him, if that’s what you mean. It’s more the voice. Like Garrett is standing just behind me, on the trail or in the painting room, and it’s so clear that my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nI’ll turn around because it feels like he’s there. And then he isn’t, obviously. I know that—well, part of me knows that. But in the moment it’s not like remembering him. It feels real, completely real.\n\nThe painting room is weird too, because the light comes in at this angle in the afternoon, and I used to always think it made everything look too yellow, and Garrett used to joke about that—sorry, I’m wandering. No, I don’t think I see anything. I hear him. That’s the part that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other sensations, like smelling or feeling something that isn't there, when you hear Garrett's voice or at any other time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the voice. I mean, I don’t think I’m smelling things or feeling hands on me or anything like that—not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that when I hear Garrett, it’s so clear, like he’s standing right behind me, on the trail or in my painting room, that my body reacts like he’s there.\n\nAnd then I turn, and—nothing. But in that second it doesn’t feel like memory. It feels real. I came in because of that, really. The voice. Garrett’s voice.\n\nDoctor: Has hearing Garrett's voice affected your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s… it’s messed things up a lot more than I wanted to admit.\n\nAt work, I couldn’t really trust myself to be in sessions safely. I’m supposed to be the steady person in the room, you know? And then I’m hearing Garrett like he’s right behind me, and I’m trying to document and I can’t keep the thread. I cancelled sessions. I fell behind on notes—badly. The paperwork just stacked up, and I’d stare at it and think about how I used to be able to organize a whole treatment plan in my head while making coffee, and now I couldn’t even tell if I was okay to be alone.\n\nAnd at home… my wife got scared. I think she was trying not to show it at first, but she ended up calling crisis services because I couldn’t trust myself by myself. That was probably the point where I couldn’t pretend it was just grief or stress anymore.\n\nBeing admitted to the hospital felt like… like everything had stopped working. Work, home, safety. All of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Sad” sounds too simple, I guess. I mean, Garrett was—he was everywhere in my life, and after he died everything felt… wrong, like the room had been rearranged but no one told me.\n\nEmpty, maybe. Sometimes. But then other times I’m not even sure what I’m feeling, because I get caught on these details, like the mug he used to use or this one bend in the trail, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should have called him that week, and then I’m not really answering your question anymore.\n\nSo… most of the time? I’m not sure. It’s been heavy. That’s the word that comes to mind. Heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you enjoyed the things you used to enjoy, like painting, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, painting used to be the thing that brought me back to myself. I’d go into that room and lose hours in a good way, you know? Now I’ll stand there with the brushes out and it’s like they belong to someone else.\n\nAnd guitar too—I used to just pick it up without thinking. Theater rehearsals, same thing. Even work, sitting with patients… those were all anchors for me. Now they feel flat. Like I’m doing the shape of my life, but the meaning isn’t there anymore.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about painting. It’s just all of it kind of went unreachable.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it down to two weeks like that. Time has been kind of… smeared together. Some days I wake up and it’s heavy right away, and other days I don’t know if I’m depressed or just tired or stuck.\n\nI keep trying to think of it like I would with one of my clients—duration, frequency, all that—but when it’s me, it gets messy. I’ll start counting days and then I’m thinking about what I was doing that week, or whether I went into the office, or if I even answered texts, and I lose the thread.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can say it’s been more than just a bad afternoon here and there, but I don’t know if I can honestly give you a clean “yes, exactly two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something to be true that you later found out was not, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “believed something to be true” can cover a lot, right? Like, after someone dies, you catch yourself thinking they’re about to walk in, or you hear a car door and for half a second your brain fills in the wrong person. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’ve had moments where I was convinced about something and then later thought, no, that doesn’t make sense, but I can’t pull out a clean example right now. My mind goes to little things—misremembering a conversation, thinking I’d sent an email when I hadn’t, that kind of thing. And then I start wondering if that counts, or if you mean something bigger.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing I can clearly point to and say, yes, that was definitely false and I fully believed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that Garrett was still alive, even when another part of you knew he was dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, sort of. Not like I sat down and decided he was alive, exactly. It’s more that I’ll be somewhere—on the trail, or in the painting room, which is ridiculous because that room is so quiet—and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, clear as anything.\n\nAnd in that second, my body believes it before my mind catches up. Like I’ll turn around expecting him to be there. Then another part of me is saying, no, he’s dead, you know this, but the voice feels so real that it almost overrides everything.\n\nI guess that’s part of why I came in. Not because I’m confused about what happened all the time, but because in those moments it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear Garrett's voice at any time, or is it only when you're in certain places like the trail or the painting room?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly in those places, I think—the trail, the painting room. Places where I guess my mind already has him… I don’t know, mapped onto everything. The trail especially, because there are spots where the sound carries strangely, and I’ll hear something and it’s just—his voice, clear, like he’s right behind me.\n\nBut then I start wondering if I’m making the place matter too much. Because it’s not like I’m calmly sitting there analyzing it. In the moment, it feels completely real. I turn like he’s standing there. And then I remember, or part of me remembers, that he’s dead.\n\nThe painting room is weird too because it’s so quiet. You’d think quiet would make things easier, but it almost gives the voice room. Anyway… I came in because it’s clear enough that I can’t just brush it off as a memory.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts organized or to express them clearly since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I can talk, obviously, but keeping it in a straight line is harder than it should be.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing—say I’m trying to write a patient note—and then suddenly that thought has turned into five different projects I should do, and then somehow I’m remembering a hike Garrett and I took, and then I’m thinking about the funeral and what I should have said or done differently. And all of that happens before I can even catch the first thought.\n\nSo when I try to explain it out loud, I can hear myself jumping around, and then I get frustrated because I know there was a point, but it’s like it moved.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear Garrett's voice when you are alone in a quiet space with no reminders of him?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there are no reminders of him, exactly. That’s the hard part—because once I’m quiet, my mind starts making reminders out of nothing, if that makes sense.\n\nBut the clearest times are the trail and the painting room. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me, and for that second I react like he is. Even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nIf I’m just in some random quiet place… I’m not sure. It’s not as clear as those places. The painting room is quiet, though, and that’s one of the main places it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking or acting as if Garrett is still alive, even when you know he's not?\n\nPatient: Yes… for a few seconds at a time. Not like I sit down and make plans with him or anything, but on the trail, or in my painting room, I’ll hear Garrett’s voice so clearly it’s like he’s standing right behind me.\n\nAnd I turn. I actually turn like I’m expecting him to be there. Then it catches up with me—like, no, that’s not possible, he’s dead. But in that moment, before the thinking part comes back online, it feels completely real. It’s strange because I know what I know, but my body reacts faster than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a thought or belief that you knew was not true, but you couldn't shake it off?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know, logically, that one person isn’t responsible for everything, and I’d tell a patient that if they were sitting across from me. I know that.\n\nBut I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett—what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I missed something—and then it branches out into work too, the sessions I cancelled, the people I let down. And then I end up with this… this belief that I failed everyone. Garrett, my patients, everyone.\n\nAnd the part I can’t shake is this feeling that people would honestly be better off if I weren’t in the way. I know that sounds extreme. I do know that. But it keeps coming back like it’s obvious, like it’s the only conclusion that makes sense, even when another part of me knows it isn’t true.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly.\n\nI keep going back over every conversation with Garrett, especially the last ones, like if I line the words up in the right order I’ll find the thing I should’ve caught. And then it doesn’t even stay with him—it spreads. I start thinking about the patient sessions I cancelled, the people who were depending on me, and I just… I convince myself I failed everyone.\n\nIt’s like this whole ledger in my head, and everything is in the red. Garrett, my patients, everyone. And then I get to this place where it feels like people would be better off if I just wasn’t in the way. I know that’s not a healthy way to think, but it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent and overwhelming sense of sadness or hopelessness for most of the day, nearly every day, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the day, nearly every day” sounds very precise, and I haven’t exactly been tracking it like a chart.\n\nThere are definitely stretches where everything feels heavy, like the light’s gone out of things. But then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like the way the paint dries too fast if the heat is on, or I’ll spend twenty minutes reorganizing brushes because one of them is in the wrong jar, and then I’m not sure if that counts as sadness or just… functioning badly.\n\nHopelessness, I don’t know. Sometimes it’s there. Sometimes it’s more like being tired of trying to make sense of it. But I can’t honestly say I know whether it’s been most of every day. I just know it’s been… around a lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes. That’s actually… that’s why I came in.

I keep hearing Garrett’s voice, not like a memory exactly. It’s clear. Like he’s standing just behind me on the trail, or in the painting room while I’m trying to work. And I’ll turn, or I’ll pause like I’m waiting for him to say something else.

I know that’s not a healthy way to think, but it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent and overwhelming sense of sadness or hopelessness for most of the day, nearly every day, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the day, nearly every day” sounds very precise, and I haven’t exactly been tracking it like a chart.\n\nThere are definitely stretches where everything feels heavy, like the light’s gone out of things. But then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like the way the paint dries too fast if the heat is on, or I’ll spend twenty minutes reorganizing brushes because one of them is in the wrong jar, and then I’m not sure if that counts as sadness or just… functioning badly.\n\nHopelessness, I don’t know. Sometimes it’s there. Sometimes it’s more like being tired of trying to make sense of it. But I can’t honestly say I know whether it’s been most of every day. I just know it’s been… around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you heard Garrett's voice and also saw him or thought he was physically present with you?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s why I came in.\n\nI keep hearing Garrett’s voice, not like a memory exactly. It’s clear. Sometimes it’s there. Sometimes it’s more like being tired of trying to make sense of it. But I can’t honestly say I know whether it’s been most of every day. I just know it’s been… around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you heard Garrett's voice and also saw him or thought he was physically present with you?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s why I came in.\n\nI keep hearing Garrett’s voice, not like a memory exactly. It’s clear. Like he’s standing just behind me on the trail, or in the painting room while I’m trying to work. And I’ll turn, or I’ll pause like I’m waiting for him to say something else.\n\nI know he’s dead. I do know that when I’m sitting here talking to you. But in the moment, it feels completely real, like he’s physically there with me. Then afterward I feel stupid for reacting to it, but at the time it doesn’t feel imaginary.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"The things that usually anchor me—painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater, even sitting with patients—feel flat and unreachable, like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I decided to stop caring. It’s more like the things that used to hold me in place—painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater— they’re all still there, but I can’t quite reach them.

I’ll pick up a brush and just stare at the canvas, or I’ll tune the guitar and then not play anything. Even being with patients, which used to feel meaningful, feels… flat now. Like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.

Sorry, I know that sounds dramatic. It’s just—I used to have these little anchors, and now they don’t anchor me. They just remind me that I’m not really in it anymore.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. Like, not just remembering it, not like when a song gets stuck in your head—actually hearing him, clear as anything, like he’s right behind me on the trail or in my painting room.\n\nAnd I know—part of me knows he’s dead. I do know that. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like a memory. It feels completely real. I’ll turn around expecting him to be there, and then… well, he isn’t. It’s been scaring me, honestly.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It sort of started after Garrett died, and since then everything’s been… unraveling, I guess. Time’s been weird, honestly—like I keep thinking it was last week and then realizing no, it’s been weeks. But yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: What is Garrett's voice saying when you hear it?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s his voice. Garrett’s voice. Clear, like he’s right there behind me—sometimes on the trail, sometimes when I’m in the painting room.\n\nI don’t know if I can even always tell you the exact words, because the part that hits me is how real it sounds. Like not a thought, not me remembering him. I’ll turn around expecting him to be standing there, and then there’s nothing. And I know he’s dead, I do know that, but in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you see or feel anything unusual when you hear Garrett's voice, or at any other time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really… see him, if that’s what you mean. It’s more the voice. Like Garrett is standing just behind me, on the trail or in the painting room, and it’s so clear that my whole body reacts before I can think.\n\nI’ll turn around because it feels like he’s there. And then he isn’t, obviously. I know that—well, part of me knows that. But in the moment it’s not like remembering him. It feels real, completely real.\n\nThe painting room is weird too, because the light comes in at this angle in the afternoon, and I used to always think it made everything look too yellow, and Garrett used to joke about that—sorry, I’m wandering. No, I don’t think I see anything. I hear him. That’s the part that scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other sensations, like smelling or feeling something that isn't there, when you hear Garrett's voice or at any other time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the voice. I mean, I don’t think I’m smelling things or feeling hands on me or anything like that—not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that when I hear Garrett, it’s so clear, like he’s standing right behind me, on the trail or in my painting room, that my body reacts like he’s there.\n\nAnd then I turn, and—nothing. But in that second it doesn’t feel like memory. It feels real. I came in because of that, really. The voice. Garrett’s voice.\n\nDoctor: Has hearing Garrett's voice affected your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s… it’s messed things up a lot more than I wanted to admit.\n\nAt work, I couldn’t really trust myself to be in sessions safely. I’m supposed to be the steady person in the room, you know? And then I’m hearing Garrett like he’s right behind me, and I’m trying to document and I can’t keep the thread. I cancelled sessions. I fell behind on notes—badly. The paperwork just stacked up, and I’d stare at it and think about how I used to be able to organize a whole treatment plan in my head while making coffee, and now I couldn’t even tell if I was okay to be alone.\n\nAnd at home… my wife got scared. I think she was trying not to show it at first, but she ended up calling crisis services because I couldn’t trust myself by myself. That was probably the point where I couldn’t pretend it was just grief or stress anymore.\n\nBeing admitted to the hospital felt like… like everything had stopped working. Work, home, safety. All of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Sad” sounds too simple, I guess. I mean, Garrett was—he was everywhere in my life, and after he died everything felt… wrong, like the room had been rearranged but no one told me.\n\nEmpty, maybe. Sometimes. But then other times I’m not even sure what I’m feeling, because I get caught on these details, like the mug he used to use or this one bend in the trail, and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I should have called him that week, and then I’m not really answering your question anymore.\n\nSo… most of the time? I’m not sure. It’s been heavy. That’s the word that comes to mind. Heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you enjoyed the things you used to enjoy, like painting, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, painting used to be the thing that brought me back to myself. I’d go into that room and lose hours in a good way, you know? Now I’ll stand there with the brushes out and it’s like they belong to someone else.\n\nAnd guitar too—I used to just pick it up without thinking. Theater rehearsals, same thing. Even work, sitting with patients… those were all anchors for me. Now they feel flat. Like I’m doing the shape of my life, but the meaning isn’t there anymore.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about painting. It’s just all of it kind of went unreachable.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it down to two weeks like that. Time has been kind of… smeared together. Some days I wake up and it’s heavy right away, and other days I don’t know if I’m depressed or just tired or stuck.\n\nI keep trying to think of it like I would with one of my clients—duration, frequency, all that—but when it’s me, it gets messy. I’ll start counting days and then I’m thinking about what I was doing that week, or whether I went into the office, or if I even answered texts, and I lose the thread.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I can say it’s been more than just a bad afternoon here and there, but I don’t know if I can honestly give you a clean “yes, exactly two weeks.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed something to be true that you later found out was not, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… “believed something to be true” can cover a lot, right? Like, after someone dies, you catch yourself thinking they’re about to walk in, or you hear a car door and for half a second your brain fills in the wrong person. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’ve had moments where I was convinced about something and then later thought, no, that doesn’t make sense, but I can’t pull out a clean example right now. My mind goes to little things—misremembering a conversation, thinking I’d sent an email when I hadn’t, that kind of thing. And then I start wondering if that counts, or if you mean something bigger.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing I can clearly point to and say, yes, that was definitely false and I fully believed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that Garrett was still alive, even when another part of you knew he was dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, sort of. Not like I sat down and decided he was alive, exactly. It’s more that I’ll be somewhere—on the trail, or in the painting room, which is ridiculous because that room is so quiet—and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, clear as anything.\n\nAnd in that second, my body believes it before my mind catches up. Like I’ll turn around expecting him to be there. Then another part of me is saying, no, he’s dead, you know this, but the voice feels so real that it almost overrides everything.\n\nI guess that’s part of why I came in. Not because I’m confused about what happened all the time, but because in those moments it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear Garrett's voice at any time, or is it only when you're in certain places like the trail or the painting room?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly in those places, I think—the trail, the painting room. Places where I guess my mind already has him… I don’t know, mapped onto everything. The trail especially, because there are spots where the sound carries strangely, and I’ll hear something and it’s just—his voice, clear, like he’s right behind me.\n\nBut then I start wondering if I’m making the place matter too much. Because it’s not like I’m calmly sitting there analyzing it. In the moment, it feels completely real. I turn like he’s standing there. And then I remember, or part of me remembers, that he’s dead.\n\nThe painting room is weird too because it’s so quiet. You’d think quiet would make things easier, but it almost gives the voice room. Anyway… I came in because it’s clear enough that I can’t just brush it off as a memory.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts organized or to express them clearly since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I can talk, obviously, but keeping it in a straight line is harder than it should be.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing—say I’m trying to write a patient note—and then suddenly that thought has turned into five different projects I should do, and then somehow I’m remembering a hike Garrett and I took, and then I’m thinking about the funeral and what I should have said or done differently. And all of that happens before I can even catch the first thought.\n\nSo when I try to explain it out loud, I can hear myself jumping around, and then I get frustrated because I know there was a point, but it’s like it moved.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear Garrett's voice when you are alone in a quiet space with no reminders of him?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there are no reminders of him, exactly. That’s the hard part—because once I’m quiet, my mind starts making reminders out of nothing, if that makes sense.\n\nBut the clearest times are the trail and the painting room. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me, and for that second I react like he is. Even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nIf I’m just in some random quiet place… I’m not sure. It’s not as clear as those places. The painting room is quiet, though, and that’s one of the main places it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking or acting as if Garrett is still alive, even when you know he's not?\n\nPatient: Yes… for a few seconds at a time. Not like I sit down and make plans with him or anything, but on the trail, or in my painting room, I’ll hear Garrett’s voice so clearly it’s like he’s standing right behind me.\n\nAnd I turn. I actually turn like I’m expecting him to be there. Then it catches up with me—like, no, that’s not possible, he’s dead. But in that moment, before the thinking part comes back online, it feels completely real. It’s strange because I know what I know, but my body reacts faster than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a thought or belief that you knew was not true, but you couldn't shake it off?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know, logically, that one person isn’t responsible for everything, and I’d tell a patient that if they were sitting across from me. I know that.\n\nBut I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett—what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I missed something—and then it branches out into work too, the sessions I cancelled, the people I let down. And then I end up with this… this belief that I failed everyone. Garrett, my patients, everyone.\n\nAnd the part I can’t shake is this feeling that people would honestly be better off if I weren’t in the way. I know that sounds extreme. I do know that. But it keeps coming back like it’s obvious, like it’s the only conclusion that makes sense, even when another part of me knows it isn’t true.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly.\n\nI keep going back over every conversation with Garrett, especially the last ones, like if I line the words up in the right order I’ll find the thing I should’ve caught. And then it doesn’t even stay with him—it spreads. I start thinking about the patient sessions I cancelled, the people who were depending on me, and I just… I convince myself I failed everyone.\n\nIt’s like this whole ledger in my head, and everything is in the red. Garrett, my patients, everyone. And then I get to this place where it feels like people would be better off if I just wasn’t in the way. I know that’s not a healthy way to think, but it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent and overwhelming sense of sadness or hopelessness for most of the day, nearly every day, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the day, nearly every day” sounds very precise, and I haven’t exactly been tracking it like a chart.\n\nThere are definitely stretches where everything feels heavy, like the light’s gone out of things. But then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like the way the paint dries too fast if the heat is on, or I’ll spend twenty minutes reorganizing brushes because one of them is in the wrong jar, and then I’m not sure if that counts as sadness or just… functioning badly.\n\nHopelessness, I don’t know. Sometimes it’s there. Sometimes it’s more like being tired of trying to make sense of it. But I can’t honestly say I know whether it’s been most of every day. I just know it’s been… around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you heard Garrett's voice and also saw him or thought he was physically present with you?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s why I came in.\n\nI keep hearing Garrett’s voice, not like a memory exactly. It’s clear. Like he’s standing just behind me on the trail, or in the painting room while I’m trying to work. And I’ll turn, or I’ll pause like I’m waiting for him to say something else.\n\nI know he’s dead. They just remind me that I’m not really in it anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean… “believed something to be true” can cover a lot, right? Like, after someone dies, you catch yourself thinking they’re about to walk in, or you hear a car door and for half a second your brain fills in the wrong person. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’ve had moments where I was convinced about something and then later thought, no, that doesn’t make sense, but I can’t pull out a clean example right now. My mind goes to little things—misremembering a conversation, thinking I’d sent an email when I hadn’t, that kind of thing. And then I start wondering if that counts, or if you mean something bigger.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Nothing I can clearly point to and say, yes, that was definitely false and I fully believed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that Garrett was still alive, even when another part of you knew he was dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, sort of. Not like I sat down and decided he was alive, exactly. It’s more that I’ll be somewhere—on the trail, or in the painting room, which is ridiculous because that room is so quiet—and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, clear as anything.\n\nAnd in that second, my body believes it before my mind catches up. Like I’ll turn around expecting him to be there. Then another part of me is saying, no, he’s dead, you know this, but the voice feels so real that it almost overrides everything.\n\nI guess that’s part of why I came in. Not because I’m confused about what happened all the time, but because in those moments it feels completely real.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear Garrett's voice at any time, or is it only when you're in certain places like the trail or the painting room?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly in those places, I think—the trail, the painting room. Places where I guess my mind already has him… I don’t know, mapped onto everything. The trail especially, because there are spots where the sound carries strangely, and I’ll hear something and it’s just—his voice, clear, like he’s right behind me.\n\nBut then I start wondering if I’m making the place matter too much. Because it’s not like I’m calmly sitting there analyzing it. In the moment, it feels completely real. I turn like he’s standing there. And then I remember, or part of me remembers, that he’s dead.\n\nThe painting room is weird too because it’s so quiet. You’d think quiet would make things easier, but it almost gives the voice room. Anyway… I came in because it’s clear enough that I can’t just brush it off as a memory.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts organized or to express them clearly since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I can talk, obviously, but keeping it in a straight line is harder than it should be.\n\nLike I’ll start with one thing—say I’m trying to write a patient note—and then suddenly that thought has turned into five different projects I should do, and then somehow I’m remembering a hike Garrett and I took, and then I’m thinking about the funeral and what I should have said or done differently. And all of that happens before I can even catch the first thought.\n\nSo when I try to explain it out loud, I can hear myself jumping around, and then I get frustrated because I know there was a point, but it’s like it moved.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear Garrett's voice when you are alone in a quiet space with no reminders of him?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say there are no reminders of him, exactly. That’s the hard part—because once I’m quiet, my mind starts making reminders out of nothing, if that makes sense.\n\nBut the clearest times are the trail and the painting room. That’s why I came in, really. I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me, and for that second I react like he is. Even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in the moment it feels completely real.\n\nIf I’m just in some random quiet place… I’m not sure. It’s not as clear as those places. The painting room is quiet, though, and that’s one of the main places it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking or acting as if Garrett is still alive, even when you know he's not?\n\nPatient: Yes… for a few seconds at a time. Not like I sit down and make plans with him or anything, but on the trail, or in my painting room, I’ll hear Garrett’s voice so clearly it’s like he’s standing right behind me.\n\nAnd I turn. I actually turn like I’m expecting him to be there. Then it catches up with me—like, no, that’s not possible, he’s dead. But in that moment, before the thinking part comes back online, it feels completely real. It’s strange because I know what I know, but my body reacts faster than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a thought or belief that you knew was not true, but you couldn't shake it off?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know, logically, that one person isn’t responsible for everything, and I’d tell a patient that if they were sitting across from me. I know that.\n\nBut I keep replaying every last conversation with Garrett—what I said, what I didn’t say, whether I missed something—and then it branches out into work too, the sessions I cancelled, the people I let down. And then I end up with this… this belief that I failed everyone. Garrett, my patients, everyone.\n\nAnd the part I can’t shake is this feeling that people would honestly be better off if I weren’t in the way. I know that sounds extreme. I do know that. But it keeps coming back like it’s obvious, like it’s the only conclusion that makes sense, even when another part of me knows it isn’t true.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly, honestly.\n\nI keep going back over every conversation with Garrett, especially the last ones, like if I line the words up in the right order I’ll find the thing I should’ve caught. And then it doesn’t even stay with him—it spreads. I start thinking about the patient sessions I cancelled, the people who were depending on me, and I just… I convince myself I failed everyone.\n\nIt’s like this whole ledger in my head, and everything is in the red. Garrett, my patients, everyone. And then I get to this place where it feels like people would be better off if I just wasn’t in the way. I know that’s not a healthy way to think, but it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent and overwhelming sense of sadness or hopelessness for most of the day, nearly every day, since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. “Most of the day, nearly every day” sounds very precise, and I haven’t exactly been tracking it like a chart.\n\nThere are definitely stretches where everything feels heavy, like the light’s gone out of things. But then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like the way the paint dries too fast if the heat is on, or I’ll spend twenty minutes reorganizing brushes because one of them is in the wrong jar, and then I’m not sure if that counts as sadness or just… functioning badly.\n\nHopelessness, I don’t know. Sometimes it’s there. Sometimes it’s more like being tired of trying to make sense of it. But I can’t honestly say I know whether it’s been most of every day. I just know it’s been… around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you heard Garrett's voice and also saw him or thought he was physically present with you?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s why I came in.\n\nI keep hearing Garrett’s voice, not like a memory exactly. It’s clear. Like he’s standing just behind me on the trail, or in the painting room while I’m trying to work. And I’ll turn, or I’ll pause like I’m waiting for him to say something else.\n\nI know he’s dead. I do know that when I’m sitting here talking to you. But in the moment, it feels completely real, like he’s physically there with me. Then afterward I feel stupid for reacting to it, but at the time it doesn’t feel imaginary.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy most of the time since Garrett passed away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not like I decided to stop caring. It’s more like the things that used to hold me in place—painting, playing guitar, rehearsing at the theater— they’re all still there, but I can’t quite reach them.\n\nI’ll pick up a brush and just stare at the canvas, or I’ll tune the guitar and then not play anything. Even being with patients, which used to feel meaningful, feels… flat now. Like I’m going through the motions of a life that stopped meaning anything.\n\nSorry, I know that sounds dramatic. It’s just—I used to have these little anchors, and now they don’t anchor me. They just remind me that I’m not really in it anymore."
  }
